If the mirror or the pictures look okay and I’ve combed my hair forward just right, I feel like I can breathe for a second. But it doesn’t really last. I start thinking about how the guests will see me under the restaurant lights, so I check again, take another picture from a different angle, and then I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs it always your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or do you worry about other parts of your body as well?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / host_or_hostess_restaurant_lounge_or_coffee_shop / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop thinking that my hairline and the way my face looks under the restaurant lights make me look old and strange, even when my coworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop thinking about how I look. Mostly my hairline, and my face under the lights at the restaurant. I feel like it makes me look old and strange. My coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t get it out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make these thoughts about your appearance go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before a shift, I keep going back to the bathroom mirror and checking it over and over. I’ll take pictures on my phone from different angles, then comb my hair forward, then check again. I keep thinking about how the guests are going to see me under the lights, and sometimes I get so caught up in it that I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your appearance over and over, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I’m trying to stop myself from looking terrible when I go out front. Like if I check the mirror enough, take enough pictures, comb my hair forward just right, maybe guests won’t notice it. But then I start replaying how they’ll see me under the lights, and I keep going until I’m late or too worked up to face people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious when you've checked your appearance and everything looks right to you?\n\nPatient: For a little while, maybe. If the mirror or the pictures look okay and I’ve combed my hair forward just right, I feel like I can breathe for a second. But it doesn’t really last. I start thinking about how the guests will see me under the restaurant lights, so I check again, take another picture from a different angle, and then I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is it always your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or do you worry about other parts of your body as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my hairline and my face. I keep thinking that under the restaurant lights I look old or strange, like it’s really obvious to people. My coworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your appearance affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’ve pulled back from people a lot. I don’t go to staff photos anymore, and I’ve skipped gallery events and hikes because I just can’t handle being seen like that. And at work it’s caused problems too—I’ve been written up because I’m late or I miss shifts when I get stuck over how I look.\n\nDoctor: Are there other areas of your life, besides work and social events, where your concerns about your appearance are causing you problems?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly those areas, but it spills over. Like, I’ve already mentioned work—I’ve been written up for being late or missing shifts when I can’t get past how I look. And outside of that, I’ve stopped doing things I used to say yes to, like staff photos, gallery events, hikes… anything where I feel like people are going to really see me. I just end up avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hairline and face actually look old and strange, or are you mostly afraid that they might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like they actually do look that way. I can’t stop thinking my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old and strange. Even when coworkers say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just end up avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hairline and face actually look old and strange, or are you mostly afraid that they might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like they actually do look that way. I can’t stop thinking my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old and strange. Even when coworkers say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people actually notice your hairline and face, or is it more that you're afraid of what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I think they actually notice it. Or… at least I can’t stop thinking they do. My hairline and my face under those restaurant lights just look old and strange to me, and even when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still be concerned about your hairline and face if you were completely alone and no one could see you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / host_or_hostess_restaurant_lounge_or_coffee_shop / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop thinking that my hairline and the way my face looks under the restaurant lights make me look old and strange, even when my coworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. I keep thinking about how the guests are going to see me under the lights, and sometimes I get so caught up in it that I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your appearance over and over, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop thinking about how I look. Mostly my hairline, and my face under the lights at the restaurant. I feel like it makes me look old and strange. My coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t get it out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make these thoughts about your appearance go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before a shift, I keep going back to the bathroom mirror and checking it over and over. I’ll take pictures on my phone from different angles, then comb my hair forward, then check again. I keep thinking about how the guests are going to see me under the lights, and sometimes I get so caught up in it that I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your appearance over and over, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I’m trying to stop myself from looking terrible when I go out front. Like if I check the mirror enough, take enough pictures, comb my hair forward just right, maybe guests won’t notice it. But then I start replaying how they’ll see me under the lights, and I keep going until I’m late or too worked up to face people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious when you've checked your appearance and everything looks right to you?\n\nPatient: For a little while, maybe. If the mirror or the pictures look okay and I’ve combed my hair forward just right, I feel like I can breathe for a second. But it doesn’t really last. I start thinking about how the guests will see me under the restaurant lights, so I check again, take another picture from a different angle, and then I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is it always your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or do you worry about other parts of your body as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my hairline and my face. I keep thinking that under the restaurant lights I look old or strange, like it’s really obvious to people. My coworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your appearance affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’ve pulled back from people a lot. I don’t go to staff photos anymore, and I’ve skipped gallery events and hikes because I just can’t handle being seen like that. And at work it’s caused problems too—I’ve been written up because I’m late or I miss shifts when I get stuck over how I look.\n\nDoctor: Are there other areas of your life, besides work and social events, where your concerns about your appearance are causing you problems?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly those areas, but it spills over. Like, I’ve already mentioned work—I’ve been written up for being late or missing shifts when I can’t get past how I look. And outside of that, I’ve stopped doing things I used to say yes to, like staff photos, gallery events, hikes… anything where I feel like people are going to really see me. I just end up avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hairline and face actually look old and strange, or are you mostly afraid that they might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like they actually do look that way. I can’t stop thinking my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old and strange. Even when coworkers say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people actually notice your hairline and face, or is it more that you're afraid of what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I think they actually notice it. Or… at least I can’t stop thinking they do. My hairline and my face under those restaurant lights just look old and strange to me, and even when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your hairline and face if you were completely alone and no one could see you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think I would still be stuck on it, even if no one else could see me. It’s not only about other people looking—it’s like I can’t stop seeing my hairline and my face that way myself, like I look old and strange. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I still do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about your appearance if everyone around you thought you looked fine?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think it would go away. I keep getting stuck on my hairline and how my face looks under the restaurant lights, like it makes me look old and strange. Even when coworkers tell me they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop thinking about how I look. Mostly my hairline, and my face under the lights at the restaurant. I feel like it makes me look old and strange. My coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t get it out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make these thoughts about your appearance go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before a shift, I keep going back to the bathroom mirror and checking it over and over. I’ll take pictures on my phone from different angles, then comb my hair forward, then check again. I keep thinking about how the guests are going to see me under the lights, and sometimes I get so caught up in it that I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your appearance over and over, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I’m trying to stop myself from looking terrible when I go out front. Like if I check the mirror enough, take enough pictures, comb my hair forward just right, maybe guests won’t notice it. But then I start replaying how they’ll see me under the lights, and I keep going until I’m late or too worked up to face people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious when you've checked your appearance and everything looks right to you?\n\nPatient: For a little while, maybe. If the mirror or the pictures look okay and I’ve combed my hair forward just right, I feel like I can breathe for a second. But it doesn’t really last. I start thinking about how the guests will see me under the restaurant lights, so I check again, take another picture from a different angle, and then I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is it always your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or do you worry about other parts of your body as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my hairline and my face. I keep thinking that under the restaurant lights I look old or strange, like it’s really obvious to people. My coworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your appearance affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’ve pulled back from people a lot. I don’t go to staff photos anymore, and I’ve skipped gallery events and hikes because I just can’t handle being seen like that. And at work it’s caused problems too—I’ve been written up because I’m late or I miss shifts when I get stuck over how I look.\n\nDoctor: Are there other areas of your life, besides work and social events, where your concerns about your appearance are causing you problems?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly those areas, but it spills over. Like, I’ve already mentioned work—I’ve been written up for being late or missing shifts when I can’t get past how I look. And outside of that, I’ve stopped doing things I used to say yes to, like staff photos, gallery events, hikes… anything where I feel like people are going to really see me. I just end up avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hairline and face actually look old and strange, or are you mostly afraid that they might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like they actually do look that way. I can’t stop thinking my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old and strange. Even when coworkers say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people actually notice your hairline and face, or is it more that you're afraid of what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I think they actually notice it. Or… at least I can’t stop thinking they do. My hairline and my face under those restaurant lights just look old and strange to me, and even when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your hairline and face if you were completely alone and no one could see you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think I would still be stuck on it, even if no one else could see me. It’s not only about other people looking—it’s like I can’t stop seeing my hairline and my face that way myself, like I look old and strange. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I still do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about your appearance if everyone around you thought you looked fine?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think it would go away. I keep getting stuck on my hairline and how my face looks under the restaurant lights, like it makes me look old and strange. Even when coworkers tell me they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance repeatedly if you were going to be alone at the restaurant, with no guests or coworkers to see you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Even if nobody was there, I’d still probably be in the bathroom checking the mirror, taking pictures with my phone from different angles, trying to comb my hair forward. I get caught up in it before a shift, replaying how I might look out front, and sometimes it makes me late or too worked up to go out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the repetitive checking of your appearance is more about fixing something that's actually wrong with how you look or about preventing something that might be wrong from being noticed by others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but mostly I’m trying to stop people from noticing what I’m afraid is wrong. Before a shift I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, take pictures from different angles, comb my hair forward, and keep replaying how guests might see me. I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I get caught up in it before a shift, replaying how I might look out front, and sometimes it makes me late or too worked up to go out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the repetitive checking of your appearance is more about fixing something that's actually wrong with how you look or about preventing something that might be wrong from being noticed by others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but mostly I’m trying to stop people from noticing what I’m afraid is wrong. I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs the feeling that you need to check your appearance repeatedly driven more by the belief that there's something actually wrong with how you look or by the fear of what others might think of you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / host_or_hostess_restaurant_lounge_or_coffee_shop / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors\nmanifestation: \"Before a shift I keep checking the bathroom mirror, taking phone pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward, and replaying how guests might see me until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

It’s more the fear of what people will think when they see me, I think. ---------- turn 14 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop thinking about how I look. Mostly my hairline, and my face under the lights at the restaurant. I feel like it makes me look old and strange. My coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t get it out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make these thoughts about your appearance go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before a shift, I keep going back to the bathroom mirror and checking it over and over. I’ll take pictures on my phone from different angles, then comb my hair forward, then check again. I keep thinking about how the guests are going to see me under the lights, and sometimes I get so caught up in it that I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your appearance over and over, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I’m trying to stop myself from looking terrible when I go out front. Like if I check the mirror enough, take enough pictures, comb my hair forward just right, maybe guests won’t notice it. But then I start replaying how they’ll see me under the lights, and I keep going until I’m late or too worked up to face people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious when you've checked your appearance and everything looks right to you?\n\nPatient: For a little while, maybe. If the mirror or the pictures look okay and I’ve combed my hair forward just right, I feel like I can breathe for a second. But it doesn’t really last. I start thinking about how the guests will see me under the restaurant lights, so I check again, take another picture from a different angle, and then I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is it always your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or do you worry about other parts of your body as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my hairline and my face. I keep thinking that under the restaurant lights I look old or strange, like it’s really obvious to people. My coworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your appearance affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’ve pulled back from people a lot. I don’t go to staff photos anymore, and I’ve skipped gallery events and hikes because I just can’t handle being seen like that. And at work it’s caused problems too—I’ve been written up because I’m late or I miss shifts when I get stuck over how I look.\n\nDoctor: Are there other areas of your life, besides work and social events, where your concerns about your appearance are causing you problems?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly those areas, but it spills over. Like, I’ve already mentioned work—I’ve been written up for being late or missing shifts when I can’t get past how I look. And outside of that, I’ve stopped doing things I used to say yes to, like staff photos, gallery events, hikes… anything where I feel like people are going to really see me. I just end up avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hairline and face actually look old and strange, or are you mostly afraid that they might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like they actually do look that way. I can’t stop thinking my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old and strange. Even when coworkers say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people actually notice your hairline and face, or is it more that you're afraid of what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I think they actually notice it. Or… at least I can’t stop thinking they do. My hairline and my face under those restaurant lights just look old and strange to me, and even when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your hairline and face if you were completely alone and no one could see you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think I would still be stuck on it, even if no one else could see me. It’s not only about other people looking—it’s like I can’t stop seeing my hairline and my face that way myself, like I look old and strange. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I still do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about your appearance if everyone around you thought you looked fine?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think it would go away. I keep getting stuck on my hairline and how my face looks under the restaurant lights, like it makes me look old and strange. Even when coworkers tell me they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance repeatedly if you were going to be alone at the restaurant, with no guests or coworkers to see you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Even if nobody was there, I’d still probably be in the bathroom checking the mirror, taking pictures with my phone from different angles, trying to comb my hair forward. I get caught up in it before a shift, replaying how I might look out front, and sometimes it makes me late or too worked up to go out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the repetitive checking of your appearance is more about fixing something that's actually wrong with how you look or about preventing something that might be wrong from being noticed by others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but mostly I’m trying to stop people from noticing what I’m afraid is wrong. Before a shift I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, take pictures from different angles, comb my hair forward, and keep replaying how guests might see me. I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check your appearance repeatedly driven more by the belief that there's something actually wrong with how you look or by the fear of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear of what people will think when they see me, I think. I do believe something looks wrong, but before a shift I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward… and then I’m just replaying how the guests are going to see me. It can go on until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I start thinking about how the guests will see me under the restaurant lights, so I check again, take another picture from a different angle, and then I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is it always your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or do you worry about other parts of your body as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my hairline and my face. I keep thinking that under the restaurant lights I look old or strange, like it’s really obvious to people. My coworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your appearance affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’ve pulled back from people a lot. I don’t go to staff photos anymore, and I’ve skipped gallery events and hikes because I just can’t handle being seen like that. And at work it’s caused problems too—I’ve been written up because I’m late or I miss shifts when I get stuck over how I look.\n\nDoctor: Are there other areas of your life, besides work and social events, where your concerns about your appearance are causing you problems?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly those areas, but it spills over. Like, I’ve already mentioned work—I’ve been written up for being late or missing shifts when I can’t get past how I look. And outside of that, I’ve stopped doing things I used to say yes to, like staff photos, gallery events, hikes… anything where I feel like people are going to really see me. I just end up avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hairline and face actually look old and strange, or are you mostly afraid that they might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like they actually do look that way. I can’t stop thinking my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old and strange. Even when coworkers say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people actually notice your hairline and face, or is it more that you're afraid of what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I think they actually notice it. Or… at least I can’t stop thinking they do. My hairline and my face under those restaurant lights just look old and strange to me, and even when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your hairline and face if you were completely alone and no one could see you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think I would still be stuck on it, even if no one else could see me. It’s not only about other people looking—it’s like I can’t stop seeing my hairline and my face that way myself, like I look old and strange. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I still do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about your appearance if everyone around you thought you looked fine?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think it would go away. I keep getting stuck on my hairline and how my face looks under the restaurant lights, like it makes me look old and strange. Even when coworkers tell me they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance repeatedly if you were going to be alone at the restaurant, with no guests or coworkers to see you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Even if nobody was there, I’d still probably be in the bathroom checking the mirror, taking pictures with my phone from different angles, trying to comb my hair forward. I get caught up in it before a shift, replaying how I might look out front, and sometimes it makes me late or too worked up to go out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the repetitive checking of your appearance is more about fixing something that's actually wrong with how you look or about preventing something that might be wrong from being noticed by others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but mostly I’m trying to stop people from noticing what I’m afraid is wrong. Before a shift I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, take pictures from different angles, comb my hair forward, and keep replaying how guests might see me. I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check your appearance repeatedly driven more by the belief that there's something actually wrong with how you look or by the fear of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear of what people will think when they see me, I think. I do believe something looks wrong, but before a shift I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward… and then I’m just replaying how the guests are going to see me. It can go on until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the need to repeatedly check your appearance more about making sure you look acceptable to yourself or about avoiding what others might think of your looks?\n\nPatient: It’s more about avoiding what others might think, honestly. I mean, I want to look okay to myself too, but before a shift I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward… mostly because I’m picturing how the guests are going to see me. Sometimes it goes on until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop thinking about how I look. Mostly my hairline, and my face under the lights at the restaurant. I feel like it makes me look old and strange. My coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t get it out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make these thoughts about your appearance go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before a shift, I keep going back to the bathroom mirror and checking it over and over. I’ll take pictures on my phone from different angles, then comb my hair forward, then check again. I keep thinking about how the guests are going to see me under the lights, and sometimes I get so caught up in it that I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your appearance over and over, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I’m trying to stop myself from looking terrible when I go out front. Like if I check the mirror enough, take enough pictures, comb my hair forward just right, maybe guests won’t notice it. But then I start replaying how they’ll see me under the lights, and I keep going until I’m late or too worked up to face people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious when you've checked your appearance and everything looks right to you?\n\nPatient: For a little while, maybe. If the mirror or the pictures look okay and I’ve combed my hair forward just right, I feel like I can breathe for a second. But it doesn’t really last. I start thinking about how the guests will see me under the restaurant lights, so I check again, take another picture from a different angle, and then I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is it always your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or do you worry about other parts of your body as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my hairline and my face. I keep thinking that under the restaurant lights I look old or strange, like it’s really obvious to people. My coworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your appearance affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’ve pulled back from people a lot. I don’t go to staff photos anymore, and I’ve skipped gallery events and hikes because I just can’t handle being seen like that. And at work it’s caused problems too—I’ve been written up because I’m late or I miss shifts when I get stuck over how I look.\n\nDoctor: Are there other areas of your life, besides work and social events, where your concerns about your appearance are causing you problems?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly those areas, but it spills over. Like, I’ve already mentioned work—I’ve been written up for being late or missing shifts when I can’t get past how I look. And outside of that, I’ve stopped doing things I used to say yes to, like staff photos, gallery events, hikes… anything where I feel like people are going to really see me. I just end up avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hairline and face actually look old and strange, or are you mostly afraid that they might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like they actually do look that way. I can’t stop thinking my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old and strange. Even when coworkers say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people actually notice your hairline and face, or is it more that you're afraid of what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I think they actually notice it. Or… at least I can’t stop thinking they do. My hairline and my face under those restaurant lights just look old and strange to me, and even when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your hairline and face if you were completely alone and no one could see you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think I would still be stuck on it, even if no one else could see me. It’s not only about other people looking—it’s like I can’t stop seeing my hairline and my face that way myself, like I look old and strange. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I still do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about your appearance if everyone around you thought you looked fine?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think it would go away. I keep getting stuck on my hairline and how my face looks under the restaurant lights, like it makes me look old and strange. Even when coworkers tell me they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance repeatedly if you were going to be alone at the restaurant, with no guests or coworkers to see you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Even if nobody was there, I’d still probably be in the bathroom checking the mirror, taking pictures with my phone from different angles, trying to comb my hair forward. I get caught up in it before a shift, replaying how I might look out front, and sometimes it makes me late or too worked up to go out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the repetitive checking of your appearance is more about fixing something that's actually wrong with how you look or about preventing something that might be wrong from being noticed by others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but mostly I’m trying to stop people from noticing what I’m afraid is wrong. Before a shift I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, take pictures from different angles, comb my hair forward, and keep replaying how guests might see me. I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check your appearance repeatedly driven more by the belief that there's something actually wrong with how you look or by the fear of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear of what people will think when they see me, I think. I do believe something looks wrong, but before a shift I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward… and then I’m just replaying how the guests are going to see me. It can go on until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the need to repeatedly check your appearance more about making sure you look acceptable to yourself or about avoiding what others might think of your looks?\n\nPatient: It’s more about avoiding what others might think, honestly. I mean, I want to look okay to myself too, but before a shift I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward… mostly because I’m picturing how the guests are going to see me. Sometimes it goes on until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of having a flawed appearance or the thought of others judging your appearance more distressing to you?\n\nPatient: Both are distressing, but the judging part hits me harder. I get stuck on the idea that my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old or strange, and then I imagine other people seeing that. I keep thinking about how the guests are going to see me under the lights, and sometimes I get so caught up in it that I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your appearance over and over, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I’m trying to stop myself from looking terrible when I go out front. Like if I check the mirror enough, take enough pictures, comb my hair forward just right, maybe guests won’t notice it. But then I start replaying how they’ll see me under the lights, and I keep going until I’m late or too worked up to face people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious when you've checked your appearance and everything looks right to you?\n\nPatient: For a little while, maybe. If the mirror or the pictures look okay and I’ve combed my hair forward just right, I feel like I can breathe for a second. But it doesn’t really last. I start thinking about how the guests will see me under the restaurant lights, so I check again, take another picture from a different angle, and then I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is it always your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or do you worry about other parts of your body as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my hairline and my face. I keep thinking that under the restaurant lights I look old or strange, like it’s really obvious to people. My coworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your appearance affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’ve pulled back from people a lot. I don’t go to staff photos anymore, and I’ve skipped gallery events and hikes because I just can’t handle being seen like that. And at work it’s caused problems too—I’ve been written up because I’m late or I miss shifts when I get stuck over how I look.\n\nDoctor: Are there other areas of your life, besides work and social events, where your concerns about your appearance are causing you problems?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly those areas, but it spills over. Like, I’ve already mentioned work—I’ve been written up for being late or missing shifts when I can’t get past how I look. And outside of that, I’ve stopped doing things I used to say yes to, like staff photos, gallery events, hikes… anything where I feel like people are going to really see me. I just end up avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hairline and face actually look old and strange, or are you mostly afraid that they might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like they actually do look that way. I can’t stop thinking my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old and strange. Even when coworkers say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people actually notice your hairline and face, or is it more that you're afraid of what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I think they actually notice it. Or… at least I can’t stop thinking they do. My hairline and my face under those restaurant lights just look old and strange to me, and even when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your hairline and face if you were completely alone and no one could see you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think I would still be stuck on it, even if no one else could see me. It’s not only about other people looking—it’s like I can’t stop seeing my hairline and my face that way myself, like I look old and strange. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I still do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about your appearance if everyone around you thought you looked fine?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think it would go away. I keep getting stuck on my hairline and how my face looks under the restaurant lights, like it makes me look old and strange. Even when coworkers tell me they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance repeatedly if you were going to be alone at the restaurant, with no guests or coworkers to see you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Even if nobody was there, I’d still probably be in the bathroom checking the mirror, taking pictures with my phone from different angles, trying to comb my hair forward. I get caught up in it before a shift, replaying how I might look out front, and sometimes it makes me late or too worked up to go out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the repetitive checking of your appearance is more about fixing something that's actually wrong with how you look or about preventing something that might be wrong from being noticed by others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but mostly I’m trying to stop people from noticing what I’m afraid is wrong. Before a shift I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, take pictures from different angles, comb my hair forward, and keep replaying how guests might see me. I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check your appearance repeatedly driven more by the belief that there's something actually wrong with how you look or by the fear of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear of what people will think when they see me, I think. I do believe something looks wrong, but before a shift I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward… and then I’m just replaying how the guests are going to see me. It can go on until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the need to repeatedly check your appearance more about making sure you look acceptable to yourself or about avoiding what others might think of your looks?\n\nPatient: It’s more about avoiding what others might think, honestly. I mean, I want to look okay to myself too, but before a shift I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward… mostly because I’m picturing how the guests are going to see me. Sometimes it goes on until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of having a flawed appearance or the thought of others judging your appearance more distressing to you?\n\nPatient: Both are distressing, but the judging part hits me harder. I get stuck on the idea that my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old or strange, and then I imagine other people seeing that. Even when coworkers say they don’t notice it, I still can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that checking your appearance repeatedly is more about fixing a flaw or making yourself feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: It’s more to make myself feel less anxious, I think. I keep trying to “fix” it—checking the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward—but really I’m trying to calm down about how the guests might see me. Sometimes I do it so long I’m late or I can’t make myself go out front.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "determine if the patient's behavior is driven by a perceived flaw in their appearance or a fear of social evaluation", "question": "Is your main goal when checking your appearance to feel better about how you look or to avoid being judged by others?"}

I can’t stop thinking my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old and strange. Even when coworkers say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people actually notice your hairline and face, or is it more that you're afraid of what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I think they actually notice it. Or… at least I can’t stop thinking they do. My hairline and my face under those restaurant lights just look old and strange to me, and even when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your hairline and face if you were completely alone and no one could see you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think I would still be stuck on it, even if no one else could see me. It’s not only about other people looking—it’s like I can’t stop seeing my hairline and my face that way myself, like I look old and strange. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I still do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about your appearance if everyone around you thought you looked fine?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think it would go away. I keep getting stuck on my hairline and how my face looks under the restaurant lights, like it makes me look old and strange. Even when coworkers tell me they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance repeatedly if you were going to be alone at the restaurant, with no guests or coworkers to see you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Even if nobody was there, I’d still probably be in the bathroom checking the mirror, taking pictures with my phone from different angles, trying to comb my hair forward. I get caught up in it before a shift, replaying how I might look out front, and sometimes it makes me late or too worked up to go out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the repetitive checking of your appearance is more about fixing something that's actually wrong with how you look or about preventing something that might be wrong from being noticed by others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but mostly I’m trying to stop people from noticing what I’m afraid is wrong. Before a shift I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, take pictures from different angles, comb my hair forward, and keep replaying how guests might see me. I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check your appearance repeatedly driven more by the belief that there's something actually wrong with how you look or by the fear of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear of what people will think when they see me, I think. I do believe something looks wrong, but before a shift I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward… and then I’m just replaying how the guests are going to see me. It can go on until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the need to repeatedly check your appearance more about making sure you look acceptable to yourself or about avoiding what others might think of your looks?\n\nPatient: It’s more about avoiding what others might think, honestly. I mean, I want to look okay to myself too, but before a shift I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward… mostly because I’m picturing how the guests are going to see me. Sometimes it goes on until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of having a flawed appearance or the thought of others judging your appearance more distressing to you?\n\nPatient: Both are distressing, but the judging part hits me harder. I get stuck on the idea that my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old or strange, and then I imagine other people seeing that. Even when coworkers say they don’t notice it, I still can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that checking your appearance repeatedly is more about fixing a flaw or making yourself feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: It’s more to make myself feel less anxious, I think. I keep trying to “fix” it—checking the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward—but really I’m trying to calm down about how the guests might see me. Sometimes I do it so long I’m late or I can’t make myself go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal when checking your appearance to feel better about how you look or to avoid being judged by others?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly to avoid being judged by other people. Before a shift I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures on my phone from different angles, combing my hair forward… and I’m basically replaying how the guests are going to see me. I do want to feel okay about how I look, but the main thing is trying to prevent that feeling that people are noticing something wrong with me. Sometimes I get so caught up in it that I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop thinking about how I look. Mostly my hairline, and my face under the lights at the restaurant. I feel like it makes me look old and strange. Before a shift, I keep going back to the bathroom mirror and checking it over and over. I’ll take pictures on my phone from different angles, then comb my hair forward, then check again. I keep thinking about how the guests are going to see me under the lights, and sometimes I get so caught up in it that I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your appearance over and over, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I’m trying to stop myself from looking terrible when I go out front. Like if I check the mirror enough, take enough pictures, comb my hair forward just right, maybe guests won’t notice it. But then I start replaying how they’ll see me under the lights, and I keep going until I’m late or too worked up to face people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious when you've checked your appearance and everything looks right to you?\n\nPatient: For a little while, maybe. Even when coworkers tell me they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance repeatedly if you were going to be alone at the restaurant, with no guests or coworkers to see you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Even if nobody was there, I’d still probably be in the bathroom checking the mirror, taking pictures with my phone from different angles, trying to comb my hair forward. I get caught up in it before a shift, replaying how I might look out front, and sometimes it makes me late or too worked up to go out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the repetitive checking of your appearance is more about fixing something that's actually wrong with how you look or about preventing something that might be wrong from being noticed by others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but mostly I’m trying to stop people from noticing what I’m afraid is wrong. Before a shift I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, take pictures from different angles, comb my hair forward, and keep replaying how guests might see me. I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check your appearance repeatedly driven more by the belief that there's something actually wrong with how you look or by the fear of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear of what people will think when they see me, I think. I do believe something looks wrong, but before a shift I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward… and then I’m just replaying how the guests are going to see me. It can go on until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the need to repeatedly check your appearance more about making sure you look acceptable to yourself or about avoiding what others might think of your looks?\n\nPatient: It’s more about avoiding what others might think, honestly. I mean, I want to look okay to myself too, but before a shift I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward… mostly because I’m picturing how the guests are going to see me. Sometimes it goes on until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of having a flawed appearance or the thought of others judging your appearance more distressing to you?\n\nPatient: Both are distressing, but the judging part hits me harder. I get stuck on the idea that my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old or strange, and then I imagine other people seeing that. Even when coworkers say they don’t notice it, I still can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that checking your appearance repeatedly is more about fixing a flaw or making yourself feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: It’s more to make myself feel less anxious, I think. I keep trying to “fix” it—checking the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward—but really I’m trying to calm down about how the guests might see me. Sometimes I do it so long I’m late or I can’t make myself go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal when checking your appearance to feel better about how you look or to avoid being judged by others?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly to avoid being judged by other people. Before a shift I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures on my phone from different angles, combing my hair forward… and I’m basically replaying how the guests are going to see me. I do want to feel okay about how I look, but the main thing is trying to prevent that feeling that people are noticing something wrong with me. Sometimes I get so caught up in it that I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is your repetitive checking of your appearance something you feel compelled to do because of how you feel about your looks, or is it something you do because of how you think others will react to your looks?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of how I think other people will react. Before a shift, I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, trying to comb my hair forward. I’m imagining how the guests are going to see me, and I keep doing it until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop thinking about how I look. Mostly my hairline, and my face under the lights at the restaurant. I feel like it makes me look old and strange. My coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t get it out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make these thoughts about your appearance go away?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before a shift, I keep going back to the bathroom mirror and checking it over and over. I’ll take pictures on my phone from different angles, then comb my hair forward, then check again. I keep thinking about how the guests are going to see me under the lights, and sometimes I get so caught up in it that I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your appearance over and over, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I’m trying to stop myself from looking terrible when I go out front. Like if I check the mirror enough, take enough pictures, comb my hair forward just right, maybe guests won’t notice it. But then I start replaying how they’ll see me under the lights, and I keep going until I’m late or too worked up to face people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious when you've checked your appearance and everything looks right to you?\n\nPatient: For a little while, maybe. If the mirror or the pictures look okay and I’ve combed my hair forward just right, I feel like I can breathe for a second. But it doesn’t really last. I start thinking about how the guests will see me under the restaurant lights, so I check again, take another picture from a different angle, and then I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is it always your hairline and face that you're concerned about, or do you worry about other parts of your body as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my hairline and my face. I keep thinking that under the restaurant lights I look old or strange, like it’s really obvious to people. My coworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still can’t stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your appearance affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’ve pulled back from people a lot. I don’t go to staff photos anymore, and I’ve skipped gallery events and hikes because I just can’t handle being seen like that. And at work it’s caused problems too—I’ve been written up because I’m late or I miss shifts when I get stuck over how I look.\n\nDoctor: Are there other areas of your life, besides work and social events, where your concerns about your appearance are causing you problems?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly those areas, but it spills over. I just end up avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hairline and face actually look old and strange, or are you mostly afraid that they might look that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like they actually do look that way. I can’t stop thinking my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old and strange. Even when coworkers say they don’t see it, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people actually notice your hairline and face, or is it more that you're afraid of what they might think if they did notice?\n\nPatient: I think they actually notice it. Or… at least I can’t stop thinking they do. My hairline and my face under those restaurant lights just look old and strange to me, and even when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I still feel like it’s obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your hairline and face if you were completely alone and no one could see you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think I would still be stuck on it, even if no one else could see me. It’s not only about other people looking—it’s like I can’t stop seeing my hairline and my face that way myself, like I look old and strange. Even when people tell me they don’t see it, I still do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about your appearance if everyone around you thought you looked fine?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think it would go away. I keep getting stuck on my hairline and how my face looks under the restaurant lights, like it makes me look old and strange. Even when coworkers tell me they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance repeatedly if you were going to be alone at the restaurant, with no guests or coworkers to see you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Even if nobody was there, I’d still probably be in the bathroom checking the mirror, taking pictures with my phone from different angles, trying to comb my hair forward. I get caught up in it before a shift, replaying how I might look out front, and sometimes it makes me late or too worked up to go out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the repetitive checking of your appearance is more about fixing something that's actually wrong with how you look or about preventing something that might be wrong from being noticed by others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but mostly I’m trying to stop people from noticing what I’m afraid is wrong. Before a shift I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, take pictures from different angles, comb my hair forward, and keep replaying how guests might see me. I can get stuck doing that until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check your appearance repeatedly driven more by the belief that there's something actually wrong with how you look or by the fear of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear of what people will think when they see me, I think. I do believe something looks wrong, but before a shift I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward… and then I’m just replaying how the guests are going to see me. It can go on until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the need to repeatedly check your appearance more about making sure you look acceptable to yourself or about avoiding what others might think of your looks?\n\nPatient: It’s more about avoiding what others might think, honestly. I mean, I want to look okay to myself too, but before a shift I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward… mostly because I’m picturing how the guests are going to see me. Sometimes it goes on until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of having a flawed appearance or the thought of others judging your appearance more distressing to you?\n\nPatient: Both are distressing, but the judging part hits me harder. I get stuck on the idea that my hairline and my face under the restaurant lights make me look old or strange, and then I imagine other people seeing that. Even when coworkers say they don’t notice it, I still can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that checking your appearance repeatedly is more about fixing a flaw or making yourself feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: It’s more to make myself feel less anxious, I think. I keep trying to “fix” it—checking the mirror, taking pictures from different angles, combing my hair forward—but really I’m trying to calm down about how the guests might see me. Sometimes I do it so long I’m late or I can’t make myself go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal when checking your appearance to feel better about how you look or to avoid being judged by others?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly to avoid being judged by other people. Before a shift I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures on my phone from different angles, combing my hair forward… and I’m basically replaying how the guests are going to see me. I do want to feel okay about how I look, but the main thing is trying to prevent that feeling that people are noticing something wrong with me. Sometimes I get so caught up in it that I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is your repetitive checking of your appearance something you feel compelled to do because of how you feel about your looks, or is it something you do because of how you think others will react to your looks?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of how I think other people will react. Before a shift, I get stuck checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures from different angles, trying to comb my hair forward. I’m imagining how the guests are going to see me, and I keep doing it until I’m late or too worked up to go out front.\n\nDoctor: Is your repetitive checking of your appearance driven by the belief that there is something fundamentally wrong with how you look, or is it driven by the fear of being evaluated or judged by others?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the fear of being judged. I do feel like something looks wrong, but what really drives me is thinking about how the guests are going to see me. Before a shift I’ll keep checking the bathroom mirror, taking pictures on my phone from different angles, trying to comb my hair forward, and I just keep going over it until I’m late or too worked up to go out front."
